Mechanizing a Proof-Relevant Logical Relation for Timed Message-Passing Protocols DOI: XXXXXXX.XXXXXXX Conference: Make sure to enter the correct conference title from your rights confirmation email; June 03–05, 2018; Woodstock, NY ISBN: 978-1-4503-XXXX-X/2018/06 CCS: Theory of computation Linear logic CCS: Theory of computation Type theory CCS: Theory of computation Process calculi
Tesla Zhang, Asher Kornfeld, Rui Li, Sonya Simkin, Yue Yao, Stephanie Balzer

Abstract
. Semantic typing has become a powerful tool for program verification, applying the technique of logical relations as not only a proof method, but also a device for prescribing program behavior. In recent work, Yao et al. 2025 scaled semantic typing to the verification of timed message-passing protocols, which are prevalent in, for example, Internet of Things (IoT) and real-time systems applications. The appeal of semantic typing in this context is precisely because of its ability to support typed and untyped program components alike—including physical objects—which caters to the heterogeneity of these applications. Another demand inherent to these applications is timing: constraining the time or time window within which a message exchange must happen. Yao et al. 2025 equipped their logical relation not only with temporal predicates, but also with computable trajectories, to supply the evidence that an inhabitant can step from one time point to another one. While Yao et al. 2025 provide the formalization for such a verification tool, it lacks a mechanization. Mechanizing the system would not only provide a machine proof for it, but also facilitate scalability for future extensions and applications. This paper tackles the challenge of mechanizing the resulting proof-relevant logical relation in a proof assistant. allowing trajectories to be interleaved, partitioned, and concatenated, while the intended equality on trajectories is the equality of their graphs when seen as processes indexed by time. Unfortunately, proof assistants based on intensional type theory only have modest support for such equational theories, forcing a prolific use of transports. This paper reports on the process of mechanizing Yao et al. 2025 ’s results, comprising the logical relation, the algebra of computable trajectories with supporting lemmas, and the fundamental theorem of the logical relation, in the Rocq theorem prover. In particular, it describes a systematic treatment of transports using commuting lemmas.



1.
Introduction

Modern computing applications are increasingly becoming
heterogeneous
, with many systems needing to interface with
foreign objects
.
These foreign objects can range from code that is externally implemented (in a language that may or may not be the same as the one internal to the system)
to actual hardware devices which communicate with the system.
An example of such an application can be found in a smart home system for monitoring air quality. The controller of the system communicates with different
sensors around the home to gather data on surrounding air temperature, humidity, pressure, etc.
Such sensors are
hardware
devices, and the controller must interact with through a protocol defined
by the manufacturer in a specification.
These protocols, in addition to prescribing program behavior, can also impose timing constraints on message exchanges between components.
In the example of the air quality system, a sensor may only provide readings after heating up an internal component for some amount of time, after which it also needs time to cool down.
The other devices in the smart home system must then comply with the time restrictions put forth by the sensor in order to behave according to the specification.

The challenge of verifying such systems against a given protocol specification is twofold: we must address both
(i)
the heterogeneity of the system and
(ii)
the timing constraints imposed by certain devices.
The heterogeneous nature of these applications means there is no common specification language we can use to describe individual components of the system, no common operational model to describe interactions between said components, and no means of composing the verification of individual parts to certify the whole.
The timing constraints mean that each of the concerns put forth by heterogeneity must be solved in a way that ensures timeliness.
Fortunately, the verification framework developed by
Yao et al. 2025
combats each of these issues.

The framework proposed by
Yao et al. 2025
utilizes

semantic logical relations

(
Constable et al. 1986
;
Martin-Löf 1982
)
,
also known as
semantic typing

(
Timany et al. 2024
)
,
to combat the heterogeneity of such systems.
Semantic logical relations permit terms
that are not necessarily (syntactically) well-typed
as inhabitants of the logical relation,
thus accommodating arbitrary foreign objects,
including sensors.
To facilitate verification of
timed message-passing
systems,
the framework uses
session types

(
Honda 1993
;
Honda et al. 1998
;
Honda et al. 2008
)

as a specification language,
which it enriches with
temporal predicates
.
Session types are behavioral types

(
Ancona et al. 2016
;
Gay and Ravara 2017
)
,
which prescribe the order in which messages must be exchanged.
The indexing with a temporal predicate additionally fixes
the point in time/time window at/during which an exchange must happen.

Yao et al. 2025
specifically chose

intuitionistic linear logic session types
(ILLST)

(
Caires and Pfenning 2010
;
Toninho et al. 2013
)
,
which, through their connection to linear logic,
provide a clear distinction between the provider and client of a given protocol.
Thus, the resulting logical relation is indexed with
timed intuitionistic linear logic session types,
referred to as
timed semantic session logical relation
(TSSLR).

The combination of linear session types and temporal predicates enable the prescription of any arbitrary timed message-passing protocol, providing a specification language for a heterogeneous system in the absence of a common one.
Crucial is the use of a
labelled transition system
(LTS)
(
Milner 1980
;
Milner 1999
;
Sangiorgi and Walker 2001
)

to express the message-passing behavior of any program or device in a system.
In an LTS, each transition on an object is annotated with an
action
that describes the willingness of that object
to engage in communication.
To accommodate timing, these transitions are also equipped with the point in time at which a message exchange may happen.
However, these time annotations are local descriptions of potential computations—in order to lift them to a global schedule,

Yao et al. 2025
introduce the notion of a
trajectory
.
A trajectory is a description of how a configuration of processes evolves over time: given an instant in time, a trajectory will produce the configuration of all components at that time.
To validate that a given trajectory is indeed the result of a sequence of timed LTS reductions,
Yao et al. 2025
introduce the notion of a
computable trajectory
, which is a pair of a trajectory and the sequence of reductions which validates it.
The logical relation is then phrased in terms of these computable trajectories, requiring that configurations of processes come equipped with some evidence that they semantically adhere to a given schedule.

The fact that inhabitants of
Yao et al. 2025
’s logical relation
not only are the actual configurations of processes
but also their associated computable trajectories—the evidence that the configuration can step from one time point to another one—makes the relation
proof-relevant

(
Benton et al. 2013
;
Benton et al. 2014
;
Sterling and Harper 2021
)
.
This evidence mirrors the communication structure prescribed by the session type,
giving rise to an
algebra
on trajectories
with operations to
interleave
(combining the trajectories of two concurrently composed process configurations),

partition
(truncating a trajectory at a given instant),
and
concatenate
(sequentially composing two trajectories).
The definition of these operations necessitates a rich equational theory on computable trajectories,
challenging any mechanization effort in a proof assistant based on intensional type theory.

In this paper, we present the first mechanization of
Yao et al. 2025
’s verification framework,
certifying protocol compliance of timed message-passing heterogeneous systems.
We carry out the mechanization in the Rocq proof assistant,
comprising the logical relation with computable trajectories and algebras as well as
a timed ILLST system with Fundamental Theorem of the Logical Relation (FTLR),
showing that well-typed ILLST processes inhabit the logical relation.
In contrast to
Yao et al. 2025
’s pen-and-paper proof,
our framework is typed and parametric in the choice of a language defining an inhabitant of the logical relation.

Contributions.

Our main contributions are

•

A mechanization of a proof-relevant logical relation for protocol compliance of timed message-passing systems;

•

A formalization of a timed ILLST system with soundness proof (FTLR);

•

A formalization of computable trajectories with commuting lemmas to combat the prolific use of transports.

Artifact

All our results were carried out in the Rocq proof assistant,
provided as an artifact with this submission.

2.
Semantic Verification Framework

In this section, we go through the definition of the verification framework as implemented in the mechanization.
Unlike
Yao et al. 2025
’s formalization,
our mechanization is typed and parametric in a process language.
Our mechanization relies on a few admitted axioms,
which we refer to in this paper as assumptions.

2.1.
Overview

The verification framework depends on the definition of three critical components:
the
computational model
, which is defined with a labelled transition system (LTS), the
protocol specification language
of
timed types for message-passing objects, and the
logical relation
.
In
Section

2.2
, we lay out the details of the labelled transition system and how it expresses both the intention and timing of a communication for a message-passing process.
Using the LTS, we establish the
process language
and how we use it as an abstraction for arbitrary components in a heterogeneous system.
In
Section

2.3
, we describe the types used to represent and verify the timed message-passing protocols.
Finally, in
Section

2.4
, we define the notion of a computable trajectory and the logical relation.

2.2.
Computational Model

In order to provide a unified specification of arbitrary objects in our system,
our framework defines a
labelled transition system
(LTS)
(
Milner 1980
;
Milner 1999
;
Sangiorgi and Walker 2001
)
.
Defining the computational model in terms of an LTS allows one to
abstractly
describe communications between different parties in a system,
making it particularly well-suited for a heterogeneous message-passing setting.
In this LTS, each transition is annotated with an
action
, which describes the readiness of an object to engage in a communication.
An action can be a readiness to
send
a message, a readiness to
receive
a message, and an empty action.
The empty action represents an actual message exchange, which occurs when two objects have
complementary
actions (i.e. a send and a receive)
and are able to proceed with communication.
The transitions are also annotated with the point in time at which the communication takes place.

2.2.1.
Actions and Time

We proceed with formally defining the LTS, beginning with
actions
.
Actions rely on the notion of a
channel
,
which is an identifier that a client (e.g. a controller) who wishes to communicate with an object (e.g. a sensor) chooses for the object.
Channels thus serve as the object’s point of contact and can be part of a message’s content, i.e. its
payload
:

Definition 1 (Payload).

A
payload
is one of the following:

•

A boolean-valued
selector
, denoted
π_{1}
or
π_{2}
, or

•

A
closing signal
denoted
()
, or

•

A
channel name

a∈\mathbb{A}
.

∎

The set
\mathbb{A}
is some countably infinite set of identifiers.

Definition 2 (Action).

An
action
is one of the following:

•

The constant
ε
, or

•

A triple
(a,d,p)
where
a∈\mathbb{A}
is a channel name,

d
is the
direction
, which can be either
!
(sending) or
?
(receiving), and
p
is a payload.

Note that the constant
ε
stands for an empty action (also known as a
silent transition
).
This corresponds to
τ
-transitions in
π
-calculus
(
Milner 1980
;
Milner 1999
;
Sangiorgi and Walker 2001
)
.
We represent the set of all actions as
\mathsf{Act}
, whose elements are represented with
\alpha
.
∎

The transitions also utilize a notion of time in their annotations, which we define through a set of axioms.
Crucial is the notion of a time
interval
(see
(
Yao et al. 2025
)
), which includes both right-bounded
[T,T^{\prime})
and
right-unbounded
[T,∞)
intervals. We decided to work with an explicitly defined
infinity
time point
∞
to
represent the unbounded end of a time interval, which is greater than any time point other than itself.

Assumption 3 (Finite Time).

We assume a set
\mathsf{time}_{\mathsf{fin}}
with a decidable, strict total order
<
.

This induces a rich structure on
\mathsf{time}_{\mathsf{fin}}
, including the existence of a
\min
and
\max
operator and
the propositional irrelevance of the
<
and
≤
relation.

Definition 4 (Time).

We extend
\mathsf{time}_{\mathsf{fin}}
to the set
\mathsf{time}
, whose elements are either an element of
\mathsf{time}_{\mathsf{fin}}
or a constant value
∞
.
We then extend the order
<
on
\mathsf{time}_{\mathsf{fin}}
to
\mathsf{time}
by stipulating that
∀t∈\mathsf{time}_{\mathsf{fin}}.~t<∞

and
¬(∞<∞)
.
∎

We maintain the same notation after extending the definition. We then have the following lemma and fact:

Lemma 5.

The order
<
on
\mathsf{time}
is still decidable and is a strict total order.

Fact 6.

Natural numbers with the usual
<
relation form a valid
\mathsf{time}_{\mathsf{fin}}
.
∎

2.2.2.
Atomic Processes

In our computational model, the smallest unit of computation is an
atomic process
,
which can communicate with other processes through channels,
akin to processes in the
\pi
-calculus
(
Milner 1980
;
Milner 1999
;
Sangiorgi and Walker 2001
)
.
So far we have been referring to such a unit colloquially as an “object.”

Definition 7 (Atomic Process).

We define a set of
atomic process
, written
\textsf{Atomic}(A)
,
which has the following elements:

•

\textsf{proc}[a]({}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\mbox{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}})
, for

and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\mbox{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}∈A
, or

•

\textsf{fwd}[a](b)
, for
a,b∈\mathbb{A}
.

∎

Each atomic process has a
providing channel
,
which is the process’ identifier (as chosen by the client) and acts as a point of contact.
The channel
a
in both
\textsf{proc}[a](\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\mbox{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}})

and
\textsf{fwd}[a](b)
is the providing channel.
The set
A
contains process terms parameterized over a providing channel. In this sense,
the elements of
A
are considered to be “nameless,” and become “named” once a providing
channel is supplied through the
\textsf{proc}[a](-)
construct.

2.2.3.
Process Language

We now define the
process language
structure, which we use to abstract over the components of a heterogeneous system. Each structure
defines a set of
nameless objects
(
\mathsf{NObj}
), which describes the “terms” in the language that are able to interact with a supplied channel,
and a
transition relation
(
\,\mathrel{\mapstochar\relbar\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\xmapsto[T]{~\alpha~}}
) on those terms, describing how named terms transition to (possibly many) other atomic processes according to some action at some time.
The choice to have
nameless
terms at the language level not only simplifies the treatment of identifiers in our development,
but also allows objects to be defined independently of a particular choice of providing channel (i.e. they are
polymorphic
in the providing channel).
The latter aspect is especially apt for our target domain. The names of the components in
heterogeneous systems often take the form of
addresses
, such as IP addresses. These addresses
are usually assigned as the system is wired up, once the components themselves have already been programmed.
Conceptually, the functionality of the components are—and should be—independent of the names of the objects.
This dictum is reflected by our use of nameless objects, which is inspired by the notion of

nameless family of configurations
in
(
Yao et al. 2025
)
.

The definition of the process language structure relies on the notion of a finite multiset, which is given first:

Assumption 8 (Finite Multisets (FMSet)).

We assume the existence of
finite multisets
, defined over a set
X
, denoted
\mathsf{FM}(X)
,
to be a finite set with possibly repeated elements drawn from
X
.
We denote it as
\mathsf{FM}(X)
, along with a disjoint union operator
\mathrel{⊎}
with unit
\varnothing
.
We assume the commutative monoid laws for
\mathrel{⊎}
and
\varnothing
.
We also assume the ability to create singleton multisets, denoted
\set{x}
for
x∈X
,
and their unions are written as
\set{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}}
for
x_{i}∈X
.

Assumption 9.

We assume the ability to extend a function
f:X\textrightarrow Y
to a function

\operatorname{\mathtt{fmap}}_{f}:\mathsf{FM}(X)\textrightarrow\mathsf{FM}(Y)
, defined as applying
f
to each element in the multiset.
This operation should preserve the monoid structure.

Definition 10 (Process Language).

We define a process language to be a structure with the following data:

•

A set of
nameless objects

\mathsf{NObj}
, which can be used as the argument of
Atomic
, and

•

A
transition relation

\mathrel{\mapstochar\relbar\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\xmapsto[\cdot]{~\cdot~}}
with the following type:

(\mathsf{NObj}×\mathbb{A})×\mathsf{Act}×\mathsf{time}_{\mathsf{fin}}×\mathsf{FM}(\textsf{Atomic}(\mathsf{NObj}))\textrightarrow\textsf{Prop}

We also define the following notation:

•

ProcLang
for the set of process language structures,

•

S⊢P\mathrel{\mapstochar\relbar\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\xmapsto[T]{~a!c~}}Ω
to say that in the language
S∈\textsf{ProcLang}{}
,
an object
P
and a set of atomic objects
Ω
satisfy the relation

\mathrel{\mapstochar\relbar\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\xmapsto[\cdot]{~\cdot~}}
, with arguments being
(a,!,c)
and
T
,

•

S⊢P\mathrel{\mapstochar\relbar\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\xmapsto[T]{~a?c~}}Ω
to say the same but with direction being
?
,

•

S⊢P\mathrel{\mapstochar\relbar\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\xmapsto[T]{~ε~}}Ω
to say the same with the action
ε
.

∎

To represent a
heterogeneous
set of components, we use the type

\sum_{S∈\textsf{ProcLang}}S.\mathsf{NObj}
. Additionally, we write:

•

\mathsf{NObj}
as a shorthand for the type
\sum_{S∈\textsf{ProcLang}}S.\mathsf{NObj}
,

•

\mathsf{Obj}
as a shorthand for the type
\textsf{Atomic}(\mathsf{NObj})
.

This
Σ
-quantification of the language means the processes can possibly be from different process languages.

With all of these definitions in place, we can now define the operational model our framework relies on:

Definition 11.

We introduce the following definitions in order to talk about the execution of processes:

•

The multiset of objects
\mathsf{Cfg}:=\mathsf{FM}(\mathsf{Obj})
, called
configurations
,
as well as the nameless version of them,
\mathsf{NCfg}:=\mathsf{Cfg}×\mathsf{NObj}
,

•

An overloaded version of multiset union
\mathrel{⊎}
extended to between
\mathsf{Cfg}
and
\mathsf{NCfg}
:

Ω_{1}\mathrel{⊎}{}\textlangle Ω_{2},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{P}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip ∎

From the definition, we can see that a nameless configuration has a distinguished
root object
,
but once instantiated, the root becomes indistinguishable from the rest.

Ω_{2}\xmapsto[T]{~a?c~}Ω_{2}^{\prime}}{Ω_{1}\mathrel{⊎}Ω_{2}\xmapsto[T]{~ε~}Ω_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{⊎}Ω_{2}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{Step-Comm}

Figure 2.1.
Configuration single-stepping rules

The transition rules in
fig.

2.1
are as follows:

(1)

Step-Obj
: Inclusion from language-specific transition,

(2)

Step-Frame
: The
frame rule
, similar to the one in
π
-calculus
(
Milner 1980
;
Milner 1999
;
Sangiorgi and Walker 2001
)
,

(3)

Step-Fwd
: Reduction of forwarding processes, and

(4)

Step-Comm
: Performs a message exchange.

If a process steps to an empty multiset, we consider it to be a process that
terminates
,
and it disappears from the environment.

\par}}_{T_{2}},{Ω^{\prime\prime}}}

Figure 2.2.
Configuration multistepping rules

The transition rules in
fig.

2.2
describe the computation of a configuration over time, where

(1)

\mathsf{refl}^{Ω}_{T}
represents a “no-op,” i.e. a transition which does not perform a computation nor progress time,

(2)

\operatorname{\mathsf{stepT}}^{Ω}_{T_{1};T_{2}}(σ)
represents a time extension on a transition. Given
σ
from
T_{2}
to
T_{3}
,

\operatorname{\mathsf{stepT}}^{Ω}_{T_{1};T_{2}}(σ)
produces a transition which starts at some earlier time
T_{1}
, with the same starting configuration
Ω
,

(3)

\operatorname{\mathsf{stepC}}^{Ω;Ω^{\prime}}_{T_{1}}(σ)
represents a progression via computation step. Given
σ
from
Ω^{\prime}
to
Ω^{\prime\prime}

and a step from
Ω
to
Ω^{\prime}
,

\operatorname{\mathsf{stepC}}^{Ω;Ω^{\prime}}_{T_{1}}(σ)
produces a new transition from
Ω
to
Ω^{\prime\prime}
without additionally progressing time.

We have the following admissible operations on multistepping,
which can be handy in constructing multistepping proofs.
They are all essential for the rest of the development.

Lemma 12 (Multistep Frame).

Proof.

By mutual-induction. In practice, we have to manually pass the induction hypothesis of the latter lemma
to the former to get the mutual induction to work.

2.3.
Protocol Specification Language

In this section, we define the language used to prescribe protocols for arbitrary timed message-passing systems.
To do this, we use
session types

(
Honda 1993
;
Honda et al. 1998
;
Honda et al. 2008
)
,
in particular
intuitionistic linear logic session types
(ILLST)

(
Caires and Pfenning 2010
;
Toninho et al. 2013
)
.
ILLST are well suited for describing the behaviors of message-passing systems
because they provide a clear distinction between client-side and provider-side actions.
In order to also account for timing constraints in these protocols, ILLST are additionally equipped with
temporal predicates
, which allow us to describe the time constraints of a protocol in addition to its intended behavior.
The grammar of
timed
ILLST is given below:

\displaystyle A,B,C::=1^{t.p}

\displaystyle\mid A_{1}⊸^{t.p}A_{2}\mid A_{1}⊗^{t.p}A_{2}

\displaystyle\mid A_{1}\mathrel{\&}^{t.p}A_{2}\mid A_{1}⊕^{t.p}A_{2}

The notation
t.p
(which we call the
temporal predicate
) contains a binder
t
for a time variable and a proposition
p
on
t
.
The scoping of time variables is best understood through the formation rules of form

\mathcal{G}⊢{A}~\textsf{type}
, where
\mathcal{G}
is the context of time variables.
We write
\mathcal{G}⊢p:\textsf{Prop}
to mean that
p
is a well-formed proposition
using the time variables from
\mathcal{G}
.
In
Yao et al. 2025
, propositions are generated from the usual propositional logic connectives
and time (in)equalities, but our development is not limited to these.

Since all binary connectives have the same scoping rule,
we will only show the rule for
1
and
⊗
:

\frac{\mathcal{G},t⊢p:\textsf{Prop}}{\mathcal{G}⊢{1^{t.p}}~\textsf{type}}\frac{\left(\mathcal{G},t⊢{A_{i}}~\textsf{type}\right)_{i∈\set{1,2}}\quad\quad\mathcal{G},t⊢p:\textsf{Prop}}{\mathcal{G}⊢{A_{1}⊗^{t.p}A_{2}}~\textsf{type}}

We define the following operator that extracts the proposition from a type
and instantiates it with time
T
. The binary cases are also similar,
so we only show the ones for
1
and
⊗
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{propOf}}(1^{t.p},T)

\displaystyle=p[T/t]

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{propOf}}(A_{1}⊗^{t.p}A_{2},T)

\displaystyle=p[T/t]

For each of the above types, the superscript
t.p
indicates the time at which a communication may occur (i.e. at a time
t
satisfying the predicate
p
), while the connective itself describes the nature of the communication.
The connectives include constructs for
sequencing
messages and
branching
. Sequencing is expressed with
the types
A_{1}⊸^{t.p}A_{2}
and
A_{1}⊗^{t.p}A_{2}
. The type
A_{1}⊸^{t.p}A_{2}
indicates that, after
receiving
a channel of type
A_{1}
,
the protocol transitions to behaving as type
A_{2}
. Dually, type
A_{1}⊗^{t.p}A_{2}
denotes the
sending
of a
channel of type
A_{1}
and proceeding as type
A_{2}
afterwards. Branching is expressed by the types
A_{1}\mathrel{\&}^{t.p}A_{2}
and
A_{1}⊕^{t.p}A_{2}
.

A_{1}\mathrel{\&}^{t.p}A_{2}

offers
a choice between channels of type
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
(i.e. it is ready to
receive
channels of either type),
and
A_{1}⊕^{t.p}A_{2}

makes
a choice between channels of type
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
(i.e. it is ready to
send
channels of either type).
The choice is conveyed by receiving and sending the labels
π_{1}
or
π_{2}
, which are boolean-valued indicators.
The type
1
denotes the terminal state of a protocol.

We illustrate timed protocol specification using timed ILLST on the example

A_{1}⊸^{t_{1}.t_{0}\leq t_{1}\leq t_{0}+15}(A_{2}⊗^{t_{2}.t_{2}=t_{1}+10}1^{t_{3}.t_{2}\leq t_{3}})

which requires a process to receive a channel of type
A_{1}
at some time
t_{1}
,
which is at most 15 units of time after an initial time
t_{0}
,
after which the process must send a channel of type
A_{2}
at some time
t_{2}
,
which is exactly 10 units of time after the receipt of the channel,
after which the process terminates.

2.4.
Logical Relation

With both the operational model and specification language in place, we may now proceed with defining the logical relation.
Before we can give the full definition, however, we must introduce the notion of a trajectory, which relates program execution states to points in time.

2.4.1.
Trajectories

We proceed with the definition of a trajectory, as well as some concrete trajectories.

Definition 18 (Trajectory).

A
trajectory

s
is a function from a time interval to
\mathsf{Cfg}
,
represented with the following type:

s:(T_{0}:\mathsf{time})\textrightarrow T≤T_{0}\textrightarrow T_{0}<T^{\prime}\textrightarrow\mathsf{Cfg}

Here,
T
and
T^{\prime}
denote the time interval
[T,T^{\prime})
, over which the trajectory is defined, where
T_{0}
is a point in time within the interval.
For brevity, when we apply a trajectory to a time
T
,
we implicitly assume that the proofs for the bound conditions are also passed and denote the application as simply
s[T]
.
We also define
\mathsf{C}(T,T^{\prime})
and
[T,T^{\prime})\textrightarrow\mathsf{Cfg}
as shorthand for the type
(T_{0}:\mathsf{time})\textrightarrow T≤T_{0}\textrightarrow T_{0}<T^{\prime}\textrightarrow\mathsf{Cfg}
.
∎

Definition 19 (Constant Trajectory).

For configuration
Ω
and times
T,T^{\prime}
, the
constant trajectory

\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{[T,T^{\prime})}(Ω)
is defined as the constant function mapping all times to
Ω
.
∎

Definition 20 (Trajectory Concat).

For
s_{1}:\mathsf{C}(T_{1},T_{2})
and
s_{2}:\mathsf{C}(T_{2},T_{3})
,
the
concatentation
of trajectories
\operatorname{\mathsf{cat}}(s_{1},s_{2}):\mathsf{C}(T_{1},T_{3})
is defined by piecing the two trajectories together.
Since time has a decidable strict order, if
T<T_{2}
at any time
T
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{cat}}(s_{1},s_{2})[T]
returns
s_{1}[T]
; otherwise, it returns
s_{2}[T]
.
∎

Definition 21 (Trajectory Extension).

For
s:\mathsf{C}(T_{1},T_{2})
,
Ω∈\mathsf{Cfg}
, and
T_{3}≤T_{1}
, we define

\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω}_{T_{3}}(s):\mathsf{C}(T_{3},T_{2})
as the extension of
s
to start at time
T_{3}

by concatenating the constant trajectory
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{[T_{3},T_{1})}(Ω)
with
s
.
∎

Definition 22 (Trajectory Partition).

For
s:\mathsf{C}(T_{1},T_{3})
and time
T_{2}
such that
T_{1}≤T_{2}<T_{3}
,
we define:

•

\operatorname{\mathsf{trajB}}_{T_{2}}(s):\mathsf{C}(T_{1},T_{2})
, the segment of
s
from time
T_{1}
to
T_{2}
(i.e. the
before
partition), and

•

\operatorname{\mathsf{trajA}}_{T_{2}}(s):\mathsf{C}(T_{2},T_{3})
, the segment of
s
from time
T_{2}
to
T_{3}
(i.e. the
after
partition).

∎

Definition 23 (Trajectory Interleave).

For
s_{1},s_{2}:\mathsf{C}(T,T^{\prime})
, the
interleave
trajectory
\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(s_{1},s_{2}):\mathsf{C}(T,T^{\prime})
is defined by
mapping
T_{0}
to
s_{1}[T_{0}]\mathrel{⊎}{}s_{2}[T_{0}]
.
∎

We then have the following properties on trajectories, which are largely corollaries of function extensionality:

Lemma 24.

For
s∈\mathsf{C}(T,T^{\prime})
, the following are true:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω}_{T}(s)

\displaystyle=s

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω}_{T_{0}}(s)[T_{1}]

\displaystyle=s[T_{1}]

\displaystyle\text{ for }T≤T_{1}<T^{\prime}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω}_{T_{0}}(s)[T_{1}]

\displaystyle=Ω

\displaystyle\text{ for }T_{0}≤T_{1}<T

Lemma 25.

The
\operatorname{\mathsf{cat}}(-,-)
operator is associative.

A number of lemmas like the above are proven and are useful for the rest of the development, but we omit them for brevity.
We refer the interested reader to the artifact.

Lemma 26 (Properties of Interleave).

We have the following commuting lemma on the
\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(-,-)
operator:

(1)

For
s:\mathsf{C}(T_{1},T^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{[T,T^{\prime})}(Ω),\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω^{\prime}}_{T}(s))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω⊎Ω^{\prime}}_{T}(\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{[T_{1},T^{\prime})}(Ω),\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω^{\prime}}_{T_{1}}(s)))

(2)

For
s:\mathsf{C}(T_{1},T^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω^{\prime}}_{T}(s),\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{[T,T^{\prime})}(Ω))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω^{\prime}⊎Ω}_{T}(\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω^{\prime}}_{T_{1}}(s),\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{[T_{1},T^{\prime})}(Ω)))

(3)

For
s_{1}:\mathsf{C}(T_{1},T^{\prime})
,
s_{2}:\mathsf{C}(T_{2},T^{\prime})
, and
T_{1}≤T_{2}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω_{1}}_{T}(s_{1}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω_{2}}_{T}(s_{2}))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω_{1}⊎Ω_{2}}_{T}(\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(s_{1},\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω_{2}}_{T_{1}}(s_{2})))

(4)

For
s_{1}:\mathsf{C}(T_{1},T^{\prime})
,
s_{2}:\mathsf{C}(T_{2},T^{\prime})
, and
T_{2}≤T_{1}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω_{1}}_{T}(s_{1}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω_{2}}_{T}(s_{2}))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω_{1}⊎Ω_{2}}_{T}(\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω_{1}}_{T_{2}}(s_{1}),s_{2}))

2.4.2.
Computable Trajectories

\frac{}{\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_refl}}(Ω):\mathcal{R}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{[T,T^{\prime})}(Ω),\mathsf{refl}^{Ω}_{T})}\frac{σ:{\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1281.62pt{\vbox to1281.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-144.80055pt\lower-1540.00587pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lxSVG@eorulefalse\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-rule} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}}_{T_{2}},{Ω^{\prime\prime}}\quad r:\mathcal{R}(s,σ)}{\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_stepC}}(r,Ω):\mathcal{R}(s,\operatorname{\mathsf{stepC}}^{Ω;Ω^{\prime}}_{T}(σ))}

Figure 2.3.
Realization relation
\mathcal{R}

For the logical relation, we are interested in trajectories that can be
realized
by a configuration multistepping
σ
.
In other words, we want to only consider trajectories which are indeed the results of valid sequences of transitions, which we call
computable trajectories
.
The realizability relation
\mathcal{R}
is defined inductively in
Figure

2.3
,
using the following proof terms:

(1)

\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_refl}}(Ω)
: The constant trajectory
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{[T,T^{\prime})}(Ω)
is validated by the reflexive transition
\mathsf{refl}^{Ω}_{T}
,

(2)

\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_stepT}}(r,T)
: Given trajectory
s
is validated by
σ
, where
σ
steps from time
T_{2}
to
T_{3}
,
the trajectory
\operatorname{\mathsf{Ext}}^{Ω}_{T}(s)
is validated by the stepping
\operatorname{\mathsf{stepT}}^{Ω}_{T;T_{2}}(σ)
, the transition which extends
σ
to start at
T

(3)

\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_stepC}}(r,Ω)
: Given trajectory
s
is validated by
σ
, where
σ
steps from configuration
Ω^{\prime}
to
Ω^{\prime\prime}
,
the trajectory
s
is also validated by
\operatorname{\mathsf{stepC}}^{Ω;Ω^{\prime}}_{T_{2}}(σ)
, which extends
σ
with the computation step from
Ω^{\prime}
onwards.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}}_{T^{\prime\prime}},{Ω^{\prime}}
,
beware of the following facts:

•

s[T]
, i.e. the init of
s
, is not necessarily
Ω
.
\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_stepC}}(r,Ω)
allows
insertion of instant steppings at the start of
σ
, while keeping the
s
unchanged. In essence,

s
describes the
most reduced form
of the stepping.

•

T^{\prime}
, the end time of
s
, is not necessarily
T^{\prime\prime}
.
σ
is allowed to finish early,
so we should expect
T^{\prime\prime}≤T^{\prime}
.

∎

We have the following lemmas on the
\mathcal{R}
relation:

Lemma 28 (
\mathcal{R}
-
<
).

If
s:\mathsf{C}(T,T^{\prime})
is realized, then
T<T^{\prime}
.

\mathcal{R}
-
<^{\prime}
).

realizes a trajectory, then

.

\mathcal{R}
also admits a number of proof rules, which are used in later developments:

Lemma 30 (
\mathcal{R}
-Frame).

\frac{r:\mathcal{R}(s,σ)}{\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_frame}}(r,Ω):\mathcal{R}(\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(r,\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{[T,T^{\prime})}(Ω)),\operatorname{\mathsf{stepF}}^{Ω}(σ))}

Lemma 31 (
\mathcal{R}
-Concat).

\frac{r_{1}:\mathcal{R}(s_{1},σ_{1})\quad r_{2}:\mathcal{R}(s_{2},σ_{2})}{\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_concat}}(r_{1},r_{2}):\mathcal{R}(\operatorname{\mathsf{cat}}(s_{1},s_{2}),\operatorname{\mathsf{stepCat}}(σ_{1},σ_{2}))}

Lemma 32 (
\mathcal{R}
-Partition).

For
s:\mathsf{C}(T_{1},T_{3})
,

\frac{σ:{\resizebox{}{447828}{\par\par\par\hbox to1281.62pt{\vbox to1281.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-144.80055pt\lower-1540.00587pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

are both
\sum
-quantified (existentially quantified but can be projected)
together with their finishing time.

Lemma 33.

The following are true:

\frac{r_{1}:\mathcal{R}(s_{1},σ_{1})\quad r_{2}:\mathcal{R}(s_{2},σ_{2})}{\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_interL}}(r_{1},r_{2}):\mathcal{R}(\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(s_{1},s_{2}),\operatorname{\mathsf{stepIL}}(σ_{1},σ_{2}))}\par\frac{r_{1}:\mathcal{R}(s_{1},σ_{1})\quad r_{2}:\mathcal{R}(s_{2},σ_{2})}{\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_interR}}(r_{1},r_{2}):\mathcal{R}(\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(s_{1},s_{2}),\operatorname{\mathsf{stepIR}}(σ_{1},σ_{2}))}

Proof.

By mutual-induction and unfolding
15
.
In practice, we have to manually pass the induction hypothesis of the latter
to the former to get the mutual induction to work.
∎

Lemma 34 (
\mathcal{R}
-Interleave).

\frac{r_{1}:\mathcal{R}(s_{1},σ_{1})\quad r_{2}:\mathcal{R}(s_{2},σ_{2})}{\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_inter}}(r_{1},r_{2}):\mathcal{R}(\operatorname{\mathsf{trajInter}}(s_{1},s_{2}),\operatorname{\mathsf{stepI}}(σ_{1},σ_{2}))}

Proof.

By
33
.
∎

For convenience, we encapsulate
\mathcal{R}
and its data into the following structure:

Definition 35 (Nameless Trajectories).

We extend the notion of trajectories to nameless configurations by replacing the codomain
from
\mathsf{Cfg}
to
\mathsf{NCfg}
, and overload the notation
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}\hss}\mbox{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{s}}[a]
for
instantiating the nameless configuration across the entire trajectory.
∎

All operations on trajectories are also defined on nameless trajectories, and they commute with the instantiation operation. These operations are omitted for brevity.
Unfortunately, the instantiation operator is overloaded with trajectory access at a particular time,
but they can be disambiguated via the type of the argument.

We now define the type
\operatorname{S}_{[T_{1},T_{2})}
of computable trajectories in terms of the relation
\mathcal{R}
.

Definition 36.

Let
\operatorname{S}_{[T_{1},T_{2})}
denote the following data, separated into two parts:

(1)

The data which are themselves nameless, including:

•

\operatorname{\pi_{\text{start}}}:\mathsf{NCfg}
for the starting point,

•

\operatorname{\pi_{\text{end}}}:\mathsf{NCfg}
for the finishing point,

•

A nameless trajectory
\operatorname{\pi_{\text{traj}}}:[T_{1},T_{2})\textrightarrow\mathsf{NCfg}
,

(2)

The data parameterized by a channel name
a∈\mathbb{A}
, including:

•

A finite time
\operatorname{\pi_{\text{stepTo}}}:\mathsf{time}_{\mathsf{fin}}
,

•

A multistepping

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par}}_{\operatorname{\pi_{\text{stepTo}}}},{\operatorname{\pi_{\text{end}}}[a]}
,

•

A realization proof
\operatorname{\pi_{\mathcal{R}}}:\mathcal{R}(\operatorname{\pi_{\text{traj}}}[a],\operatorname{\pi_{\text{σ}}})
.

The
\pi
notations are all projection functions, and they are invoked with or without a channel name depending on the kind of data being projected.
For example, for
w:\operatorname{S}_{[T_{1},T_{2})}
, we would write
\operatorname{\pi_{\text{start}}}(w)
for the starting configuration of
w
and
\operatorname{\pi_{\text{σ}}}(w,a)
for the multistepping on channel
a
in
w
.
The latter is invoked with the channel name because it comes from the collection of parameterized data in
\operatorname{S}_{[T_{1},T_{2})}
.
Furthermore, we will write
w[T]
for
\operatorname{\pi_{\text{traj}}}(w)[T]
.
∎

The two-part separation in the definition of
\operatorname{S}_{[T_{1},T_{2})}
arises from the fact that, while the multistepping and
\mathcal{R}
relations
are defined for named configurations, we want to be able to access nameless configurations, as those are the inhabitants of the logical relation.
Storing the nameless and named data separately allows us to conveniently access these nameless configurations without necessarily needing to provide a channel name.

We now define some operations on computable trajectories which are essential to our development:

Definition 37 (Constant).

For
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\mbox{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}:\mathsf{NCfg}
and times
T,T^{\prime}
,
define
\mathsf{S\_refl}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\mbox{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}):\operatorname{S}_{[T,T^{\prime})}
such that:

∀a,~\operatorname{\pi_{\mathcal{R}}}(\mathsf{S\_refl}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\mbox{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}),a)

.
The rest of the fields are immediate.
∎

Definition 38 (Partition).

We define both of the following:

•

For
w:\operatorname{S}_{[T_{1},∞)}
and
T_{1}≤T_{2}
,
define
\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_partB}}(w,T_{2}):\operatorname{S}_{[T_{1},T_{2})}
such that:

\operatorname{\pi_{\mathcal{R}}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_partB}}(w,T_{2}),a)
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_partB}}(\operatorname{\pi_{\mathcal{R}}}(w,a),T_{2})
.

•

For
w:\operatorname{S}_{[T_{1},T_{3})}
and
T_{1}≤T_{2}<T_{3}
,
define
\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_partA}}(w,T_{2}):\operatorname{S}_{[T_{2},T_{3})}
such that:

\operatorname{\pi_{\mathcal{R}}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_partA}}(w,T_{2}),a)
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_partA}}(\operatorname{\pi_{\mathcal{R}}}(w,a),T_{2})
.

In both definitions, the rest of the fields are immediate.
∎

Fact 39.

For
w:\operatorname{S}_{[T_{1},∞)}
, if
T_{1}≤T<∞
, then

\operatorname{\pi_{\text{end}}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_partB}}(w,T))=w[T]
.

Also,
\operatorname{\pi_{\text{end}}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_partB}}(w,∞))=\operatorname{\pi_{\text{end}}}(w)
.
∎

Fact 40.

For in-bound
T^{\prime}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_partB}}(w,T)[T^{\prime}]=w[T^{\prime}]
.
∎

Fact 41.

\operatorname{\pi_{\text{start}}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_partA}}(w,T))=w[T]
.
∎

Definition 42 (Concat).

For
w_{1}:\operatorname{S}_{[T_{1},T_{2})}
and
w_{2}:\operatorname{S}_{[T_{2},T_{3})}
such that

\operatorname{\pi_{\text{end}}}(w_{1})=\operatorname{\pi_{\text{start}}}(w_{2})
,
define

\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_concat}}(w_{1},w_{2}):\operatorname{S}_{[T_{1},T_{3})}

such that

∀a,~\operatorname{\pi_{\mathcal{R}}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_concat}}(w_{1},w_{2}),a)
is

\operatorname{\mathsf{R\_concat}}(\operatorname{\pi_{\mathcal{R}}}(w_{1},a),\operatorname{\pi_{\mathcal{R}}}(w_{2},a)).

∎

Definition 44 (Communication).

Definition 45 (Frame).

Given
w:\operatorname{S}_{[T,T^{\prime})}
, define the following two operations:

•

For
Ω:\mathsf{Cfg}
, define

\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_frame}}(w,Ω):\operatorname{S}_{[T,T^{\prime})}

such that

\operatorname{\pi_{\text{start}}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_frame}}(w,Ω))=Ω\mathrel{⊎}\operatorname{\pi_{\text{start}}}(w)
and

\operatorname{\pi_{\text{end}}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_frame}}(w,Ω))=Ω\mathrel{⊎}\operatorname{\pi_{\text{end}}}(w)
.

to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\mbox{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}))=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\mbox{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\mathrel{⊎}\operatorname{\pi_{\text{end}}}(w)[a]
.

∎

The frame operation is a special case of the following interleaving operation:

Definition 46 (Interleave).

For
w_{1}:\operatorname{S}_{[T,T^{\prime})}
,
w_{2}:\operatorname{S}_{[T,T^{\prime})}
, and
a∈\mathbb{A}
,
define
\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_inter}}_{a}(w_{1},w_{2}):\operatorname{S}_{[T,T^{\prime})}
such that for all in-bound
T^{\prime\prime}
,

\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_inter}}_{a}(w_{1},w_{2})[T^{\prime\prime}]=w_{1}[T^{\prime\prime}][a]\mathrel{⊎}w_{2}[T^{\prime\prime}].

∎

2.4.3.
Logical Relation

With the definition of computable trajectories in hand, we may now proceed with defining the logical relation.
The
logical relation
describes the intended runtime behavior of configurations for a given type and point in time,
prescribing what it means for a configuration to be an “inhabitant” and thus to comply with the protocol prescribed by the type and imposed temporal predicate.

For
w:\operatorname{S}_{[T_{0},∞)}
,

w∈\mathcal{E}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{\text{left}}\mathrel{@}{T}

\iff

∀T^{\prime},~\operatorname{\mathsf{propOf}}(A,T^{\prime})∧T≤T^{\prime}⇒w[T^{\prime}]∈\mathcal{V}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{\text{left}}\mathrel{@}{T^{\prime}}

w∈\mathcal{E}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{\text{right}}\mathrel{@}{T}

\iff

∀T^{\prime},~\operatorname{\mathsf{propOf}}(A,T^{\prime})⇒T≤T^{\prime}∧w[T^{\prime}]∈\mathcal{V}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{\text{right}}\mathrel{@}{T^{\prime}}

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\mbox{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}∈\mathcal{V}\llbracket 1\rrbracket^{d}\mathrel{@}{T}

\iff

∀a,~\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\mbox{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}[a]\xmapsto[T]{~a!()~}\varnothing

(5)

Figure 2.4.
Definition of logical relation

fig.

2.4
gives the definition of the logical relation in terms of
two mutually recursive “interpretations,”
\mathcal{E}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{d}\mathrel{@}{T}
and
\mathcal{V}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{d}\mathrel{@}{T}
,
often referred to as the “term” (or expression, computation) and “value” interpretations of type
A
at time
T
, respectively.
For both interpretations, the annotation
d
indicates whether the configuration is being classified as a
provider
of protocol
A
or to be used by a
client
at type
A
at time
T
, with
d=\text{right}
representing the former and
d=\text{left}
the latter.
The inhabitants of the term interpretation are elements
w
of
\operatorname{S}_{[T_{0},∞)}
, which represent nameless configurations equipped with computable trajectories validating their semantic adherence to a schedule in the interval
[T_{0},∞)
(for some time
T_{0}
).
Elements
w
are in the term interpretation
\mathcal{E}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{d}\mathrel{@}{T}
if, for all times
T^{\prime}
satisfying the temporal predicate at
A
, the configuration in the trajectory at time
T^{\prime}
satisfies the value interpretation
\mathcal{V}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{d}\mathrel{@}{T^{\prime}}
.
Depending on the modality
d
, one will either need to show
T\leq T^{\prime}
as a premise (
d=\text{left}
) or conclusion (
d=\text{right}
).
The inhabitants of the value interpretation are nameless configurations
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
Ω
, and they are in the value interpretation of
A
if they are ready to send or receive a message and thus engage in an
external communication
.
The value interpretation is defined by structural induction on the type,
specifying for each type what the expected runtime behavior of an inhabitant is.

In terms of notation,
Yao et al. 2025
uses
\mathcal{L}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{\star}\mathrel{@}{T}
and
\mathcal{V}\llbracket A\rrbracket\mathrel{@}{T}

for
\mathcal{E}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{\text{left}}\mathrel{@}{T}
and
\mathcal{V}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{\text{right}}\mathrel{@}{T}
, respectively.

We now highlight some notable cases of the value interpretation, explaining how it captures the behavior specified in
section

2.3
at each type.

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-0.29999pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{-0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{-0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{-0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.29999pt\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.27715pt\raisebox{0.11479pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.21211pt\raisebox{0.21211pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.11479pt\raisebox{0.27715pt}[0.0pt]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}\hss}\mbox{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{Ω}}∈\mathsf{NCfg}

The relation is closed under pre-composition of stepping and passage of time:

Lemma 47 (Backwards Closure).

For
w_{1}:\operatorname{S}_{[T,T^{\prime})}
,
w_{2}:\operatorname{S}_{[T^{\prime},\infty)}
with
\operatorname{\pi_{\text{end}}}(w_{1})=\operatorname{\pi_{\text{start}}}(w_{2})
,
then
w_{2}∈\mathcal{E}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{\text{right}}\mathrel{@}{T^{\prime}}\implies\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_concat}}(w_{1},w_{2})\in\mathcal{E}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{\text{right}}\mathrel{@}{T}
.

Lemma 48 (Forwards Closure).

For
w∈\mathcal{E}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{\text{left}}\mathrel{@}{T}
, and
T\leq T^{\prime}
, then

\operatorname{\mathsf{S\_partA}}(w,T^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{\text{left}}\mathrel{@}{T^{\prime}}
.

3.
Fundamental Theorem

In this section, we verify arbitrary well-typed programs in a given type system using the logical relation.

3.1.
Overview

To perform this mode of verification, we need to develop a type system that is strong enough to ensure that any
well-typed term behaves as prescribed by the logical relation. The proof of this property is referred to as the
fundamental theorem of the logical relation

(FTLR). By carrying out the proof of the FTLR “once-and-for-all,” per-program verification reduces to a typechecking problem—if it typechecks, we can simply invoke the FTLR to get our desired result.
Additionally, if typechecking is decidable, the FTLR will make program verification automatic.
In
section

3.2
, we will introduce the necessary definitions for the language we want to verify, including the terms, types, typing rules, and semantics.
In
section

3.3
, we use the above language definitions to define the fundamental theorem for well-typed terms, as well as an adequacy result that allows us to verify that inhabitants of the logical relation behave as expected.

3.2.
Language Definition

In this exploration, we will consider a language of process terms which can be specified with the types defined in
section

2.3
.
The grammar for this language is defined in
Figure

3.1
,
with the terms in the left column being constructors for the corresponding behavioral types in the right column.
The grammar contains boolean-valued signals
e
(with
π_{1}
being
false
and
π_{2}
being
true
), symbols
s
(which can either be channel names
a∈\mathbb{A}
, where
\mathbb{A}
is a countably infinite set of identifiers, or a variable
x
),
finite points in time
T
, and process terms
M
.
Each of the terms is annotated with either a time
T
or a temporal predicate
t.p
(as introduced in
section

2.3
).
Binding occurrences are denoted by
x⇒M
.
As detailed in the next section, we will type these process terms using
timed ILLST
(
Caires and Pfenning 2010
;
Toninho et al. 2013
;
Toninho 2015
)
.

Figure 3.3.
Retyping rules.

To type the process terms defined in
Figure

3.1
we use
session types

(
Honda 1993
;
Honda et al. 1998
;
Honda et al. 2008
)
, and in particular
timed intuitionistic linear logic session types
,
as discussed in
Section

2.3
.

To type the process terms in
Figure

3.1
, we use a typing judgment of the form

\mathcal{G};\mathcal{F}\mid Γ⊢M\mathrel{@}T::A,

which can be read as “the process term
M
provides the communication behavior (i.e. “session”) of type
A
at time
T
under typing context
Γ
, temporal variable context
\mathcal{G}
, and assuming truth of propositions in
\mathcal{F}
.” In this judgment,
Γ
provides the typing of free variables in
M
,

\mathcal{G}
provides the scoping of time variables, and
\mathcal{F}
. Note that the contents of
\mathcal{G}
are accessible in the contexts
\mathcal{F}
and
Γ
, the time
T
, and the type
A
.
The inference rules for this judgment are defined in
Figure

3.2
.

The rules in
Figure

3.2
are given in a
sequent calculus
,
describing the behavior of a session from the point of view of a provider of the session—expressed by so-called
right rules
—as well as from the point of view of a client—expressed by so-called
left rules
.
Computationally, the rules can be read bottom-up,
where the type of the conclusion denotes the protocol state of the object
before
the message exchange,
and the type of the premise denotes the protocol state
after
the message exchange.
As a consequence, since the conclusion is asserted at time
T
, the premises must take place at some future time.
The rules are thus in agreement with the behavior specified in
section

2.3
at each type.
For example, in case of the right rule
⊕
-Right
, the provider sends either of the labels
π_{1}
or
π_{2}
at time
T

and then transitions to offering the session
A_{i}
at any time
t
satisfying the temporal predicate
p
, for
i∈\set{1,2}
.
On the other hand, in case of the left rule
⊕
-Left
, the client branches on the received label at time
T
,
continuing with either
M₁
or
M₂
at a chosen time
T^{\prime}
such that
T^{\prime}
satisfies the temporal predicate
p
.

While the typing judgment uses a context
Γ
similarly to the
λ
-calculus,
the typing context here contains a list of
bindings
, which we represent as a finite map

Γ:\textsf{var}\rightharpoonup\textsf{Types}
, where

var
is the set of variables and
Types
is the set of types.
We do use the conventional
λ
-calculus notation
Γ,x:A
for the extension of contexts,
but this should be interpreted as inserting the key-value pair
x\mapsto A
into the map
Γ
.

The rules
Cut
and
ID
both carry an additional premise,
written
A⋉A^{\prime}\mathrel{@}T
and
A⋊A^{\prime}\mathrel{@}T
, respectively.
These are the
retyping
relations, which are well-explained in
Yao et al. 2025
[§4.1].
The rules for retyping are defined in
Figure

3.3
.

3.2.2.
Runtime Process Terms

At runtime, we assign channel names (i.e. elements of the set
\mathbb{A}
) to variables in the context.
Such an assignment is called a
substitution

σ:\textsf{var}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{A}
from variables to names.
These are not to be confused with substitutions in the
λ
-calculus,
in that we are only substituting channel names for variables, not other terms with possibly free variables.
As a result, the possibility of incurring capture is ruled out in the first place.

Similar to the
λ
-calculus, we write
σ,b/x
for extending the substitution
σ
with the key-value
pair
x\mapsto b
, where
b
is a channel name.
Additionally, we write
σ(x)
for obtaining the name assigned to the variable
x
from
σ
.

We define runtime process terms by applying a substitution to process terms
M
, which is defined in
Figure

3.4
.
Notably, the operation
σ-x
is used for removing
x
from the substitution
σ
.

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{fwd}}^{T}(\textleftarrow x))

=

\operatorname{\mathsf{fwd}}^{T}(\textleftarrow σ(x))

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{let}}^{T}~x:A\textleftarrow M₁;M₂)

=

\operatorname{\mathsf{let}}^{T}~x:A\textleftarrow\hat{σ}(M₁);\widehat{σ-x}(M₂)

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{t.p}())

=

\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{t.p}()

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{recv}}^{T}_{x}();M)

=

\operatorname{\mathsf{recv}}^{T}_{σ(x)}();\widehat{σ-x}(M)

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{recv}}^{t.p}(y⇒M))

=

\operatorname{\mathsf{recv}}^{t.p}(y⇒\widehat{σ-y}(M))

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{T}_{x}(M_{1});M_{2})

=

\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{T}_{σ(x)}(\hat{σ}(M_{1}));\hat{σ}(M_{2})

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{t.p}(M_{1});M_{2})

=

\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{t.p}(\hat{σ}(M_{1}));\hat{σ}(M_{2})

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{recv}}^{T}_{x}(y⇒M))

=

\operatorname{\mathsf{recv}}^{T}_{σ(x)}(y⇒\widehat{σ-x}(M))

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{T}_{x}(π_{i});M)

=

\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{T}_{σ(x)}(π_{i});\hat{σ}(M)

(for
i∈\set{1,2}
)

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{t.p}(π_{i});M)

=

\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{t.p}(π_{i});\hat{σ}(M)

(for
i∈\set{1,2}
)

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{recv}}^{t.p}(π_{1}⇒M₁\mid π_{2}⇒M₂))

=

\operatorname{\mathsf{recv}}^{t.p}(π_{1}⇒\hat{σ}(M₁)\mid π_{2}⇒\hat{σ}(M₂))

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{recv}}^{T}_{x}(π_{1}⇒M₁\mid π_{2}⇒M₂))

=

\operatorname{\mathsf{recv}}^{T}_{σ(x)}(π_{1}⇒\hat{σ}(M₁)\mid π_{2}⇒\hat{σ}(M₂))

Figure 3.4.
Substitution of terms in the process language.

Remark 49.

As is usual, we define
\hat{σ}
by structural induction on the untyped process term,
removing any bound variable encountered (
σ-x
).
This choice is pretty standard; alternatively we could have updated the substitution
σ

by mapping the bound variable to itself.

If we were to consider typed terms instead of raw terms,
we could exploit linearity,
the question comes up how
\hat{σ}
should handle free variables in our type system.
For instance, consider the typing rule for
⊗
-Right

with scoping-related premises explicitly written out:

\frac{\left(\mathcal{G},t;\mathcal{F},p\mid Γ_{i}⊢M_{i}::A_{i}\right)_{i∈\set{1,2}}\quad\mathcal{G},t;\mathcal{F},p⊢T≤t\quad Γ₁∩Γ₂=\varnothing}{\mathcal{G};\mathcal{F}\mid Γ₁∪Γ₂⊢(\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{t.p}(M_{1});M_{2})\mathrel{@}T::(A_{1}⊗^{t.p}A_{2})}\quad\textsc{{$⊗$-Right}}

By linear typing, we know that the contexts
Γ₁
and
Γ₂
are disjoint,
which indicates a disjoint splitting of
σ
exists, say
σ=σ₁∪σ₂
,
where
σ_{i}
and
Γ_{i}
have the same domain for
i∈\set{1,2}
.
If we were to define
\hat{σ}
for well-typed terms, then it seems more appropriate to define instead:

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{t.p}(M_{1});M_{2})=\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{t.p}(\widehat{σ₁}(M_{1}));\widehat{σ₂}(M_{2}).

However,
fig.

3.4
defines
\hat{σ}
for this case as:

\hat{σ}(\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{t.p}(M_{1});M_{2})=\operatorname{\mathsf{send}}^{t.p}(\hat{σ}(M_{1}));\hat{σ}(M_{2}).

This is much easier to implement, but a few lemmas need to be proved about it.
As of now, we are unclear about which approach is favorable.
∎

We then have the following lemmas on substitution:

Lemma 50 (Composition of Substitution).

For any term
M
,
x∉σ
, and channel name
b∈\mathbb{A}
:

\widehat{b/x}(\hat{σ}(M))=\widehat{σ,b/x}(M)

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
M
and expanding the definition of
\hat{σ}
.
∎

Before proceeding to
54
and
56
,
we define the following, which extends binary relations to elementwise relations on finite maps.
The goal is to related contexts and substitutions—both are finite maps from variables.

Definition 51.

We say two finite maps
m_{1},m_{2}:X\rightharpoonup Y
are
related

by a relation
R:Y×Y\textrightarrow\textsf{Type}
when for every
x∈X
,
one of the following holds:

•

m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are both undefined at
x
,

•

m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are both defined at
x
and
R(m_{1}[x],m_{2}[x])
holds.

∎

Definition 52.

We introduce the following notations:

•

For
m:X\rightharpoonup Y
and
f:Y\textrightarrow Y^{\prime}
,
we write
f(m)
for applying
f
to the values of
m
, without changing the keys.

•

For
m:X\rightharpoonup Y
and
x:X
,
we write
m-x
for removing the key
x
from
m
.

∎

Lemma 53.

For disjointed maps
m_{1},m_{2}
, if
m_{1}⊎m_{2}
are related with
σ
, then we can compute
a disjoint decomposition
σ_{1},σ_{2}
such that
m_{i}
are related with
σ_{i}
for
i∈\set{1,2}
,
and
σ=σ_{1}⊎σ_{2}
.

A convenient way to represent the facts that two maps have the same domain is to
say that they are related by any suitable relation, so the above facts can be used to derive
other useful propositions about these maps. For example,
we can define the following lemma for related type context maps
Γ
and substitution maps
σ
:

Lemma 54 (Discard).

For
\mathcal{G};\mathcal{F}\mid Γ⊢M\mathrel{@}T::A
and substitutions
σ,σ^{\prime}
such that
σ
and
Γ
are related maps,
and
σ
disjoint with
σ^{\prime}
,
\widehat{σ\cup σ^{\prime}}(M)=\hat{σ}(M)
.

Proof.

By induction on the typing of
\mathcal{G};\mathcal{F}\mid Γ⊢M\mathrel{@}T::A
and
53
.
∎

Definition 55.

We implement a process language (

),

,

The following stepping rules always apply:

({\operatorname{\mathsf{fwd}}^{T}(\textleftarrow b)},a)

\mathrel{\mapstochar\relbar\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\xmapsto[T]{~ε~}}

\textsf{fwd}[a](b){}

\textsf{proc}[b^{\prime}](M_{1})\mathrel{⊎}{}\textsf{proc}[a](\widehat{b^{\prime}/x}(M_{2}))

, for

,

for universally quantified

\mathrel{\mapstochar\relbar\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\xmapsto[T]{~a!π_{i}~}}

Before we can state the FTLR, we need to define closing substitutions for the typing context

,

δ
, and implement as a finite map from

is
a computable trajectory with its providing channel

, and the

connects variables from

to these trajectories.
At runtime, these variables are substituted with channel names,
so we need a way to guarantee that these connected trajectories are also “well-behaved”, as dictated by the logical relation:

Definition 56.

We define the set of
complementary configurations
for a context
Γ

at a given time
T
as
δ:\text{vars}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{A}×\operatorname{S}_{[T,∞)}
such that

δ
and
Γ
are related by the relation
s∈\mathcal{E}\llbracket A\rrbracket^{\text{left}}\mathrel{@}{T}
, for
(a,s)∈δ
and
A∈Γ
.

The proposition that
δ

is a complementary configuration for
Γ
is written as
\operatorname{\mathsf{typedCompl}}(Γ,δ)
.
∎

Applying the first projection to
δ
obtains a substitution,
which we will denote by
\underline{δ}
.
We note here that this operation commutes with insertion, union, and deletion on finite maps, which are all mechanized.

The following definition describes the operation of “applying” complementary configurations
δ

to a nameless atomic process
